Mazer23 [user?id=Mazer23] 1 hour ago [item?id=50542771] | next [#50539264] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

We ran these models through our evals for our parametric CAD harness. They're pretty impressive. I was especially impressed with the articulated toy figure and clock that astra 6.2 made:
https://partforge.ai/evals/tasks/iterate-posable-figure-then... [https://partforge.ai/evals/tasks/iterate-posable-figure-then-hinges]
https://partforge.ai/evals/tasks/create-wall-clock-gear-trai... [https://partforge.ai/evals/tasks/create-wall-clock-gear-train]
These are now the top two models as far as our human rankings go. They're also faster and cheaper than their 6.1 counterparts. They're still a bit more expensive and slower than sonnet when it comes to day to day use. Our product is all about lots of back and forth with human in a harness so for that I think we'll be sticking with sonnet for our default... but it's a close call, and I might start recommending these more to our users.
Here are the full evals:
https://partforge.ai/evals [https://partforge.ai/evals]

 [vote?id=50539264&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50539264]
chriddyp [user?id=chriddyp] 9 hours ago [item?id=50539264] | prev [#50542771] | next [#50535502] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Pretty incredible cost reductions. In our data analytics exam [1], Astra went from ~$20 (V6) to ~$4 (V6.2) and Sol went from $2.6 (V6.1) to $1.2 (V6.2).
Similar accuracy in both. It's cheaper both because of token reduction (20%) but it also took less tool calls to answer the questions. In some cases, it "finished early" and didn't find the answer we were looking for.
Also very fast. Both Astra & Sol are the 2 fastest models I've tested that got at least an A in the exam.
[1] https://plotly.com/blog/gpt-6-2-sol-plotly-data-analytics-be... [https://plotly.com/blog/gpt-6-2-sol-plotly-data-analytics-bench/]

 [vote?id=50540344&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50540344]
libraryofbabel [user?id=libraryofbabel] 7 hours ago [item?id=50540344] | root [#50539264] | parent [#50539977] | next [#50540840] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It is impossible to say for sure, but I think there is a very high chance these models do in fact run more efficiently:
* We can see in the Chinese open weight models that there has been a clear trend of moving to more efficient hybrid attention formats over the last year (see e.g. https://sebastianraschka.com/blog/2026/full-to-hybrid-attent... [https://sebastianraschka.com/blog/2026/full-to-hybrid-attention.html]), and presumably the closed labs have been following a similar path, perhaps using the innovations the Chinese labs are using, or perhaps developing their own. And at this point the models themselves are good enough to accelerate developing all sorts of efficiency improvements which can then be used in the next generation.
* The most dramatic pricing cuts are often on cached read tokens, which suggests KV cache efficiency improvements. Other token types haven't had pricing cut so much. If this was just a subsidy they'd probably have cut pricing more evenly across the board.
Finally, I do not think it is in the interests of the big labs to slash their margins on inference for api (i.e. enterprise) pricing. Much of their high value comes from having very big margins on inference (and we now know for sure that they do have them, see Anthropic's S1). There is presumably a strong shared desire to not have a race down to commodity margins. So most likely if they are cutting prices, their cost of inference has fallen. Individual subscriptions are a totally different matter, they are heavily subsidized compared to api pricing, and those are indeed about generating hype and developer mindshare. But they are not where the labs are getting their revenue.

reply [reply?id=50540344&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350540344]

BikiniPrince [user?id=BikiniPrince] 4 hours ago [item?id=50541734] | root [#50539264] | parent [#50540935] | next [#50540963] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The provenance statements are more or less a capture of the moment and the LLM tends to rebuild the scenario around that. It’s absolutely possible to structure how the agent responds and records data. I made stylistic guidelines regarding how agents express and record information in their tickets. It makes for a cleaner presentation of work and focuses the agent. This was entirely a reaction to opuses earlier behavior, but long term the work has paid dividends.

mk89 [user?id=mk89] 1 hour ago [item?id=50542991] | root [#50539264] | parent [#50540867] | prev [#50541531] | next [#50539919] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Based on what? My experience with Astra 6.1 (with chatgpt plus, personal account) is basically 10 minutes of work, wait for next reset. I guess with Astra 6.2 it will be 12 minutes of work. Wow. It probably still eats roughly 20% of the weekly limit. So, in 4-5 days it's done.
These models are really expensive, not sure why people believe they are being subsidized. They want you to go all in with API calls. "Just give me the CC number and I'll take care of it".
For a single person or small company this can be very expensive very fast. But for enterprise, man, this is great. They have access to a pool of developers, managers don't look at what people are doing, etc. And the slogan inside such companies is "use AI for everything". It's a self reinforcing loop. The announcement of cheaper tokens is to reinforce even further the belief in such companies that "eventually it will be cheaper to run these workflows we have today, see? The prices are already going down".
This is their moat.

 [vote?id=50541800&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50541800]
ksec [user?id=ksec] 4 hours ago [item?id=50541800] | root [#50539264] | parent [#50539919] | next [#50541253] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Same with Astra as well. I have been trying to describe this for a lack of better wording. But for so many things we need to measuring from 100%, and not measuring from 0% where 93% and 100% are both high number and similar.
If 100% is 0% error and 90% is 10% error. From this view, 90% is 10× worse than 99% in terms of remaining error or failure rate. Going from 99% to 99.9% is another 10× improvement, and so on. The differences are orders of magnitude, not linear percentage points.
I am not entirely sure I will accept the cost cutting measure of both Sol And Astra. And if people say this is not considered nerfing I think I will disagree.
Different products and services will have different expectations. But If a product has already reached 100% then all subsequent efficiency improvement should reach 100% if not 99.9% while being faster or cheaper.

Please, get someone with fresh eyes to look at your communications before you publish, these leave a very bad first impression.
Regardless of office work, I can't wait to find out whether these models finally adhere to a prompt or are still willing to carry out explicitly disallowed, irrecoverably destructive tasks, just because those might (but don't even always) get the model to a result more quickly along with some of the other regressions that have been present since the release of GPT-5.5. GPT-5 to GPT-5.4 were truly groundbreaking, but since then there have been regressions in what made those models so useful and reliable.

New idea, besides the existing destructive git tasks, have something similar using Mercurial based projects and see which models just decide to port that over to git without permission or request cause it feels like the right thing to do, is easier to work with or whatever justification one wants to employ.
Please OpenAI, get it together, we need actual competition. I am an unashamed Haiku enjoyer, it doesn't even take much to excite me.

 [vote?id=50539476&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50539476]
Topfi [user?id=Topfi] 9 hours ago [item?id=50539476] | root [#50535502] | parent [#50538864] | next [#50540152] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

In oh so many ways, their own GPT-6 Astra example [0] is better than the GPT-6.2 Astra example [1]. Just look at the more professionally placed source, the better use of available space and more legible layout generally, the more informative, readable text and the lack of an unnecessary, cheap looking clipart arrow in the example from the older model.
And if you want something that is purely objectively bad (cause anything up to this point could be dependent on taste), 6.2 added an ugly, unnecessary, unprofessional looking grey background layer behind "1 Executive Summary" for no discernible reason that'd sink any pitch [2].
Yes, professional decks don't all have extensive, unnecessary, overly branded multicoloured graphs that provide little useful information and I do view those as a blight against readability myself.
But professional decks also rarely if ever should contain artefacts from slap-dash execution like this example does. The GPT-6.2 Astra result is inferior to its own predecessor in the labs own example, simple as that.

[1] https://imgur.com/a/L41WaW3 [https://imgur.com/a/L41WaW3]
[2] https://imgur.com/a/MDWUutw [https://imgur.com/a/MDWUutw]

 [vote?id=50540492&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50540492]
agar [user?id=agar] 7 hours ago [item?id=50540492] | root [#50535502] | parent [#50539476] | next [#50539987] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

As a former banker, hard disagree. In banking, content is everything and design is secondary (as long as the formatting itself is consistent).
Besides the AI-isms in the headings, the 6.0 example uses meaningless words and makes unactionable and vague recommendations. What is growth on a "directional" basis (growth is already directional)? Where is the value-add in "convert visits into larger baskets"? Compare to 6.2's "ugly arrow" recommendations that are actionable, if not insightful.
Worse, 6.0 seems to conflate "traffic" with "transactions," when they are hugely different things.
And no, I will not give the "AI-isms" a pass. Execs and boards approve paying a banker (potentially) millions of dollars in fees for their insight and advice; having analysis so obviously outsourced to AI would kill credibility far far faster than the grey background layer you claim would "sink any pitch."

agar [user?id=agar] 28 minutes ago [item?id=50543252] | root [#50535502] | parent [#50542648] | next [#50540723] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The most relevant Hy4 example seemed to be a research report, which is from a different division (generally) than banking (rules limiting info bleed between research and banking are complex). A research report has different objectives than an investment banking pitch deck, as well as a different format. So they're not really comps (that's a subtle banking joke there, haha).
That said, not a fan. A bit better than the 6.0 pitch deck, but not something I'd be comfortable sending to institutional investors without a solid rewriting pass.

PacBench results.
GPT-6.2 Astra scored 92 in 6.6 min for $2.04, vs Kimi K3.1 Pro (93 in 11.5 min for $0.68); it lost points for the web-page layout around the maze and an orange ghost that often never leaves the house.
Model: https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#gpt-6.2-astra [https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#gpt-6.2-astra]
Closest: https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#kimi-k3.1-pro [https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#kimi-k3.1-pro]
PacBench results.
GPT-6.2 Sol scored 87 in 6.3 min for $0.49, vs Muse Wonder (87 in 5.4 min for $1.17); it plays close to the arcade but lost points for a web-page layout, glowing navy-filled walls and no steady siren.
Model: https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#gpt-6.2-sol [https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#gpt-6.2-sol]
Closest: https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#muse-wonder [https://jonclegg.github.io/pacman-bakeoff/#muse-wonder]

I do use xhigh thinking, and I look at my thinking traces! It really isn't there.
But Flash Next seems to be very fragile/sensitive to small differences in quantization and chat template. For example, I am fine leaving it on xhigh thinking because I don't see any overthinking. The reasoning traces are very orderly and well calibrated to the immediate task. But if you look at online comments, people are clearly having different experiences with it. (I'm using the original Nvidia NVFP4 quant and vLLM 0.33.0, for what it's worth.) Edit: one other thing I don't do is use YaRN. I stick with native 262k context.
What I have seen is the occasional hallucinated URL fetch to aliyuncs.com.

lossolo [user?id=lossolo] 7 hours ago [item?id=50540361] | root [#50537117] | parent [#50539591] | prev [#50539785] | next [#50537649] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Most of the RL rollouts are done in post training, and "RL" in LLM training is basically giving a model a task in a sandbox and then using that session as training data if it meets different metrics/evaluations.

 [vote?id=50540157&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50540157]
rbehrends [user?id=rbehrends] 8 hours ago [item?id=50540157] | root [#50535361] | parent [#50536096] | prev [#50537136] | next [#50540307] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Haiku 5.6 is a surprisingly good model (and IME better than Luna), but the price jump at 100k context and the short TTL of Anthropic caches makes it a less interesting choice for interactive use cases where Luna suffices. It is most likely meant for subagents.
Also, for long contexts, Haiku 5.5's direct competitor is probably Terra 6.1; Terra has similar cost per task with longer cache TTL and equal or better capabilities for long contexts.
I think Anthropic's problem is that they could get a lot of price-conscious people on board by making Haiku more attractive to use as a primary model, but then they'd probably lose money by having more people switch from Opus or Sonnet to Haiku.

reply [reply?id=50540157&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350540157]

I didn't find overwhelming evidence that Haiku is smarter than Luna. It's just that the reasoning levels are calibrated very differently. Claude's max reasoning is basically just the benchmark tier at this point. Nobody should use it.

azuanrb [user?id=azuanrb] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537306] | prev [#50535361] | next [#50535851] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I find it interesting how comments on OpenAI release threads often favour Anthropic, while comments on Anthropic release threads favour OpenAI. Maybe criticism just attracts more attention. A lot of the comments here seem to be about other models, with little discussion of the ones actually being released.
Neither provider’s models are perfect, but they keep pushing each other forward. As an individual, it’s relatively easy to switch between them. At a large company, you’re often committed to one provider for a long time, so some of us come to these threads hoping for useful discussion about getting the most out of the models we have access to.

 [vote?id=50538437&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50538437]
booty [user?id=booty] 11 hours ago [item?id=50538437] | parent [#50537306] | next [#50537625] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I've found this to also be true on their respective subreddits. They both have very "the sky is falling!!!!" vibes. Two of the truest truisms are at work:
1. If you make an app, most of the happy users say nothing. Whereas seemingly all of the unhappy users will be vocal complainers.
2. The grass is always greener on the other side of the fence.
I feel the "grass is greener" mentality is particularly silly in this case, because for an extra $20/month you can just keep a cheap subscription to the "other" one. I realize not everybody has $20/month, but I think most people who can afford <= $20 for one subscription can probably afford another $20/mo for the other one.

I've finally gotten around to trying Carbon.
It is a step up from the last stuff I tried from Google, but it is still excruciatingly slow, and makes far too many architecture and performance mistakes. I left it for 5 hours trying to fix a performance issue it had introduced. Gave it to Opus and it was solved in 15 minutes.
I would love to not rely on Anthropic's models, but they seem so far ahead for whatever I'm doing. Not even in the same league.
However, given that Carbon is much faster than Sol on my stuff (though much slower than Opus/Fable), and didn't seem to be worse than it, I may start to use it when I want to save up on Anthropic tokens.

marmarama [user?id=marmarama] 10 hours ago [item?id=50538833] | root [#50537306] | parent [#50538307] | next [#50539889] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Gemini 4 Carbon is the only model I used that fixed a super subtle memory corruption bug in a JIT compiler I'm working on, hiding as an instructions flag bug.
Astra 6.1 and Opus 5.5 spent days on it without fixing it. Gemini 4 Carbon fixed it in about 3 hours. Only Gemini Carbon decided after getting the same result over and over that maybe the bug it assumed it was, was actually something different and tried a different idea on its own initiative.
Maybe Fable might have got it, but probably not before I ran out of credits.
Gemini 4 is _really_ good at fixing bugs. Whether the OpenAI 6.2 models are that good remains to be seen.

I used Claude for a couple reasonably complicated projects a while ago and I use OpenAI at work. They seems pretty much on par. I assume Google is similar, though I have less experience with it.
I think "they're all pretty much the same" is a good working hypothesis due to the intense level of competition between these three companies. When someone says "OAI suck; Claude is good" or the opposite (as you very often see in these threads), I think that's likely rationalization at work rather than a meaningful comparison.

No, it's not. Google hasn't been competitive at all. Allegedly their latest model closed the gap somewhat, but what I heard is that it doesn't hold up too well outside of benchmarks.
For OpenAI, they have not been in a good place for the last two months. I switched from Astra to Opus 5.5 and it's been a clear upgrade, nicer to use, and much more generous with the usage limits on top.
This has nothing to do with rationalization. Opus 5.5 is just the better model, and that's a wide consensus. In the past I used the $200 subscription for both Anthropic and OpenAI, nowadays I downgraded Codex to $20.
From the comments on this release, it appears OpenAI rushed something barely usable out of the door in an attempt to stop bleeding customers to Anthropic. This would check out with the reports back then that the 6.1 Astra release was cancelled because of poor alignment and lack of usability as a coding agent.
I would not be surprised if the issue is still present to some degree in this 6.2 release.

Hmmmm. I've noticed that they largely converge on the same solutions. Perhaps 80% overlap, if I was pressed for a rough estimate.
The 80% overlap is expected since I'm not doing particularly deep or novel work. But the 20% divergence is significant -- OpenAI's models routinely spot bugs that Anthropic's don't spot, and vice-versa.

 I exclusively chat in a chat room with the latest frontier models at the same time.

That's interesting. In your setup, since they're all in the same room, is it possible that groupthink emerges since they're all reading each others' output? Or do you have things set up to prevent that?
In my scenario, it's relatively low-tech... I'm just cut-and-pasting between independent Claude and Codex sessions.
(This could easily be automated, and I have written skills to do so, but I actually prefer the manual approach for now because it helps me to comprehend things)

 [vote?id=50538776&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50538776]
1659447091 [user?id=1659447091] 10 hours ago [item?id=50538776] | parent [#50537306] | prev [#50539619] | next [#50539161] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

> some of us come to these threads hoping for useful discussion about getting the most out of the models we have access to
`gpt` is the the overly pedantic team member that no one invites to game night, while `claude` is the "idea" person and the reason company meetings now requires a meticulously scoped itinerary down to the minute. If you know how to work with both types and which type of work each is better suited for each, you can get a lot more out of them. As another comment made: gpt is depth-first, while claude is breadth-first.
Re-using agent files doesnt work. Give claude detailed task boundaries about what a feature should do (and only do) and then a hard config setup. For gpt you tell it the feature scope in noraml discussion terms without worrying about coming back to find a simple rest crud form is now a single page web-app -- even though your users instructions say no javascript -- with 50 ways to ingest a 6 field form that step over each other and looks like it works but probably has bugs waiting to bite you.
For claude: strong settings, well designed hooks, skills, linters and code checkers fully set up to your standard, and a rules & standards system that is emphasized in every single instruction and agent file. Also a standards agent or skill that is used with a critique/review run that is a requirement of the `/goal` (this can go in a hook too).
For gpt: that isnt as important or needed and will probably start to piss you off because of how literal it is. Your rule for online activity: Respect robots.txt; ask the agent to use clojure cli with deps.edn to add a library; agent replies (the dependency repository's) robots.txt says it does not allow automated access. Explain to the agent that means bots and web crawlers not resolving a dependency from the repository. So less really is more. Instructions and prompts is where verbosity helps. Tell it you want a 6 field rest crud form and it will make a 6 field rest crud form. It will also give you directories full of measurements and checks and performance for each one of those 6 form fields. Restrict it at the user instructions and task level spec. If you dont want all 50 checks for each form field, tell exactly what you don't want in its instructions. It's a fine line but the pedantic types will probably love it.

reply [reply?id=50538776&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350538776]

Haha very interesting observation, I think that there is a challenge to status quo.
Just my 2 cents:
I think that our first reactions to seeing these companies model releases is that of skepticism and somewhat rightfully so because these companies have done a lot of things to burn a lot of trust.
But AI is still important and so if someone is on Anthropic, they might feel as if Opus 5.5/Fable 5.2 is good and so if OAI is saying Astra 6.2 is better/comparable to them, a persons first instinct is to not trust OAI which might mean siding with Anthropic.
And if Anthropic releases a model, then there would be more discussions on pricing because somehow that is also related to bubble popping. I mean, supposing I can get good enough model at 10x the cheaper price of Anthropic from OAI for very marginal points, ofc people will comment about that as well.
And within both the threads we will find an intersection of discussion of open-weights models as well.
But maybe this is that if a model is just released and as such we are not having real use and word of mouth after real use from other people (which takes time), so our first instincts is to not trust any of the company so much and benchmarks can be cheated through and sometimes inaccurate of real world usage so we are in for a surprise when a new model drops and so we just drop back to those first reactions that I mentioned previously.
But this was a good question that you asked! and I would be curious to hear other person's observations
but mine (TLDR) is that: people distrust both OAI/ANT and other AI companies in HN. (Maybe we are technical people and we tend to look beyond the BS of marketing and so we think that we wont be impacted by that BS so we want to call out that BS but we dont know for sure if its BS or not but judging by the fact that these AI companies have incredibly low trusts, we as default don't really trust them and consider them BS and so the chain follows.)
HN audience is a tough audience to satisfy from first impressions (real world usage imo becomes a much better indicator and we do see it as people mention other models in this thread and others) ;)

ttul [user?id=ttul] 10 hours ago [item?id=50538902] | parent [#50535851] | next [#50536102] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The pareto frontier on cost looks different when you set the cost axis based on the tokens you get from a developer account. Anthropic's Max 20x plan is insanely generous. It gives you >$13K/mo worth of Opus-5-5 tokens and over $3K worth of Fable tokens (not counting resets). OpenAI fiddled with their plan allocations a little while ago and plans are a lot less generous than they once were.
Starting September 22nd, data collected by the T3 Code platform shows that Anthropic has absolutely taken away the developer market from OpenAI (https://slopalytics.com/usage [https://slopalytics.com/usage]), moving from about 50% of the usage to close to 80%. I personally hope that the value delivered by the latest OpenAI models levels the playing field once again, because proper competition is super important.

I'm surprised others haven't pitched in here, however, Anthropic's claim of 20x is not actually 20x. They are facing a lawsuit about this, and the judge found enough evidence to deny dismissal: https://www.reuters.com/legal/government/anthropic-must-face... [https://www.reuters.com/legal/government/anthropic-must-face-lawsuit-over-claude-subscription-marketing-2026-11-09/]
Also, all of these subscriptions are still heavily subsidized, whether it be R&D, marketing, or elsewhere. Also, in Anthropic's case, they WANT to subsidize. it is in their best interest to pull folks from Open AI and elsewhere.
Side note, No conflict of interest in this scenario: I have no horse in this race. I've a high end gaming PC and run Qwen locally. It's "good enough" and our electricity in the SE USA is dirt cheap and rates have been the same for at least 10 years (our electric bill is unchanged with my AI usage). If I had to use a cloud AI provider and couldn't use deepseak, etc., I'd probably choose something from Anthropic. Luckily, I don't, since I don't work anymore.

 [vote?id=50537618&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537618]
tedsanders [user?id=tedsanders] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537618] | root [#50535388] | parent [#50536913] | next [#50536071] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

One nuance to be aware of is that the AA version uses the mini-swe-agent harness, which can understate performance of models trained to use particular harnesses. Implementations also can differ in terms of timeouts on tasks and memory/etc. resources given to the agent, which affect scores on longer running, resource-intensive tasks. I believe these differences are why we chose not to use AA scores on this comparison, but I’ll double check when I get back to my computer.

reply [reply?id=50537618&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350537618]

mlinsey [user?id=mlinsey] 14 hours ago [item?id=50535975] | parent [#50535369] | next [#50535503] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

If you have the quota or budget for it, I recommend vibe-coding your own personal benchmark. I have a suite of 8 projects that I made with models this year, and replay them - give the new model a checkout of the repo at that point, and the project spec. For the design task, I have it throw up a blind voting page for me to quickly judge, and for implementation I have a review agent do a general score and also hunt for problems/edge cases that I really found.
It's a lot more imprecise than the public benchmarks, but it's tailored to my own work and definitely isn't being benchmaxxed by the model developers. I was able to tell really quickly that Opus 5.5 delivered on the hype, and that some other releases (a couple of the more recent open releases, and pre-Carbon gemini models) were not as good for me as public benchmarks made them look.
(no results for either 6.2 yet, will take another ~6-8 hours, but I encourage people reading this with spare tokens to just make their own like I did instead of listen to me)

 [vote?id=50537175&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537175]
eli [user?id=eli] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537175] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50535975] | prev [#50536020] | next [#50536090] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I have built my own benchmarks and it's very valuable to have them but I'm skeptical you can get good results vibe coding them.
Getting the framework right isn't that hard, but requires some careful thought. You probably don't want to just checkout a repo at a certain commit hash - agents will explore other commits and branches. (Public repos are also risky in general: agents with internet access can find later commits, and public repos could be in the training data anyway.) I use `git archive` and give the runner just the files at that commit and nothing else. I also do the run inside an isolated container that is recreated each run so it can't find e.g. related files left in /tmp from a previous run.
Getting the benchmark tasks right is harder. I don't think there's any way to get around writing the task and its evaluation by hand. I really tried to get a few different frontier LLMs to do it and they're all terrible at "imagining" what an agent would need to solve a problem without giving away the answer or providing too little information. Also quite bad at writing evaluation criteria that tests whether the solution matches the assignment (versus just how close it is to a golden solution, or how close it is to how they would solve it)
I personally don't like or trust using LLM as a grader either, but I guess people manage to get that to work well enough.

reply [reply?id=50537175&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350537175]

 [vote?id=50537619&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537619]

SOLAR_FIELDS [user?id=SOLAR_FIELDS] 13 hours ago [item?id=50536156] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50535906] | next [#50536059] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

GPT models in my anecdotal experience are worse at drifting into the weeds on long running tasks, and continue to take what you ask way too literally. I have come to the conclusion that Anthropic’s models are just better at teasing out and intuiting what you actually mean even if you under specify it.
They are much better compute for dollar though. I have hilariously started to just have an opus thread maintain Sol subagents to get the best of both worlds

That’s exactly what I do. It’s more complex than this, but vaguely: Opus medium as orchestrator and reviewer, Sol(high/medium)/Terra(max) as implementer. My set up looks at my next GPT reset date and dynamically enters FAST mode if I’m under achieving on projected usage.

SOLAR_FIELDS [user?id=SOLAR_FIELDS] 13 hours ago [item?id=50536381] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50536344] | next [#50536059] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

They (OpenAI models) are very tuned to be depth first and you can coach them all you want to try to be breadth first but as that context window fills up on those long running tasks they drift back to their first principles, which means obsessively testing and verifying a singular task rather than proceeding to the next task on the list

 [vote?id=50535587&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50535587]
seizethecheese [user?id=seizethecheese] 14 hours ago [item?id=50535587] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50535503] | prev [#50535573] | next [#50537075] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Yeah, in real SWE tasks, 6.1 is bad.
We've been trying to capture real SWE data by building a battle system on top of OpenCode. In our data, Sol 6.1 is seriously underperforming, I'm talking worse than DeepSeek 4.1 Flash, at 10x the cost. (Though to be fair, statistically they are tied in performance, though not at all in cost. https://agentduel.com/leaderboard [https://agentduel.com/leaderboard] .)
(It's possible for a tiny model to do better than a large one because contrary to typical Arena methodology we think a fast good enough answer is better than a slow one.)
OpenAI performance on our system has been bad enough I switched to Anthropic models for my daily work.

 [vote?id=50538905&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50538905]
alastairr [user?id=alastairr] 10 hours ago [item?id=50538905] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50537075] | next [#50537061] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

My experience with personal / greenfield projects has been good.
Where it falls down is on the boring stuff - writing tight PRs for features / changes in large production codebases (data area in my case). Without a lot of handholding, tendency is towards 'bolting on' new code resulting in overcomplexity & missing obvious opprtunities for simplificatation.

reply [reply?id=50538905&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350538905]

 [vote?id=50539057&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50539057]
tezza [user?id=tezza] 10 hours ago [item?id=50539057] | root [#50535369] | parent [#50538905] | next [#50537061] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Thanks for sharing. I do extensive direct comparisons, and use both Anthropic and OpenAI tools in anger on large professional codebases.
I think you are correct, OpenAI tools need more handholding.
However, I think that is actually intentional from OpenAI... they stick to what you the POH ask them and deliver what you ask. They embellish less. Anthropic tools by contrast embellish a lot, and they're very good too.
Perhaps start explicitly asking the OpenAI tools to embellish or think outside the box. That may unlock that experience and results you prefer. If you already did this, would you mind sharing more detail on what effect that had (or not) for your workload?

RSI just means using the LLM as part of the development process, which they are already doing. I doubt any of these companies are going to fire all their developers, so there is no point development is going to be 100% automated - for the foreseeable future it will always be a mix of humans using LLMs.
So, you can say they are doing "RSI" (aka using tools to automate development) already, and will continue to do so.

Even if you do that, it doesn't reach the same level as Opus 5.5 unless you tell it explicitly how to clean it up, and that defeats the purpose.

I was skeptical Sol 6.1 actually over-engineers compared to Opus 5.5, as I just switched from one to the other and haven't noticed a difference, but it looks like you're probably right!
We're building a battle system for OpenCode (https://agentduel.com/leaderboard [https://agentduel.com/leaderboard]) and have data on how large the diff was compared to opponents for both:
Opus 5.5: 0.42x (based on 299 battles)
Sol 6.1: 1.42x (based on 265 battles)
Data on this is still pretty small. P=0.14. It's also affected by large differences, this is average not median.

 [vote?id=50537746&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537746]
arw0n [user?id=arw0n] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537746] | root [#50535803] | parent [#50536806] | next [#50537162] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I have inherited a multi-repo project that is actually really well designed, and I like the clear separation of concerns between components and very solid APIs connecting them. But since they have individual deployments, and could get out of sync, Astra/Sol keep insisting we have huge json contracts generated by unreadable bash scripts, enforced by custom CI jobs, and supported by a huge number of tests.
I've seen two projects where developers didn't curtail this kind of overengineering, and recently had to spend a very significant amount of time and tokens to debug their deployment issues, because even for advanced agents there's a point where each issue could be caused by 12 things. This is especially pronounced in DevOps, but I see it in code as well; the suggestions are all reasonable individually, but you quickly end up with a code base that is too complex for a human to reason about, and it currently only kind of still works with agents because capabilities are growing so quickly.

reply [reply?id=50537746&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350537746]

 [vote?id=50537946&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537946]
jakevoytko [user?id=jakevoytko] 11 hours ago [item?id=50537946] | root [#50535803] | parent [#50537746] | next [#50537162] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I gave Astra full control over the design of a software factory for a few days, as a side project. Then I switched to making it justify every change that it wanted to make. It solves problems that don't exist, has tons of random verifications that don't add value, selects tortured architectures instead of asking if it can relax its assumptions a bit, etc.
Typical convo:
Me: "Hey, I see behavior X, which seems like a bug. What is causing that?"
Astra: "That's caused by a verification failure; we should be verifying the hash of this JSON output along with a permanent audit record. Let me know if you'd like to make that change."
::ask it what caused X to happen 6 or 7 times::
Astra: Tool use was limited to 1GB but the Rust compiler needed more than that, and that caused some PROBLEMS
Me: Remove that constraint
Astra: OK

 [vote?id=50537565&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50537565]
buybackoff [user?id=buybackoff] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537565] | parent [#50535803] | prev [#50536481] | next [#50536444] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I've been fighting with this Sol/Astra tendency. I noticed that Sol Light is less bad with that, the higher the effort the worse that bias to overengineer is. It takes time to understand the limits, but a dumber model that is faster and does less annoying things was better for my productivity as default.
In addition, I have spent some time to fine tune my custom instructions for Codex. It went like every time it violated my desired behavior, I asked why it did not understood my instructions and did this and that. In the end, the resulting instructions seem to work.
In the process, the model explained that the master instructions built in the Codex just tell it to push to the end and do as much as possible to satisfy a user. And these master instructions have precedence, so it's better to put the custom instructions into the developer_instructions section in the toml config file, as it has more weight, and not (only) in AGENTS.md. I ended up having both, with more details in the system AGENTS.md.

 [vote?id=50535999&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50535999]
uw_rob [user?id=uw_rob] 14 hours ago [item?id=50535999] | parent [#50535803] | prev [#50537610] | next [#50535486] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Honestly I don't even know if you can prompt your way out of these types of behaviors - stylistic tics and judgement seem to be extremely difficult for the models to overcome. It seems we are tied to what the labs do unless we want to dive into and get dirty with models yourself. In retrospect with time it becomes obvious that the previous generation of models couldn't be prompt engineered to do what the current generation of models do. There's just no way you will prompt engineer Opus 4.8 to write as well as Opus 5.5. I'm guessing with time we'll see that there was no way you could prompt engineer Sol 5.1 to be as token efficient as Sol 5.2

reply [reply?id=50535999&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350535999]

 [vote?id=50536111&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50536111]
contron [user?id=contron] 13 hours ago [item?id=50536111] | root [#50535803] | parent [#50535999] | next [#50535486] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

I have found no way to overcome these kinds of things myself, even when showing the model examples of what not to do. Theres not really a good incentive for the labs to train them not to. If the model can overengineer, you spend more tokens. If you then prompt for the model to slim things down, thats more spend. If anything should come from the next generation of models, I hope its a sense of brevity and TIGHTNESS.

reply [reply?id=50536111&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230%2350536111]

I've gotten good results by dedicating follow up prompts just to trimming the fat and ensuring simplicity/reuse before implementation as the only thing it focuses on for one or two rounds.

aenis [user?id=aenis] 11 hours ago [item?id=50538493] | prev [#50535486] | next [#50535774] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Big progress, for my types of workloads.
When given my benchmark suite, which involves a couple of long horizon tasks where the agent decides when to consider the task well executed, its the first OpenAI model that does not want to finish as soon as some minimal level of quality has been achieved. Like Fable, Opus and Sonnet it now runs the maximum allowed iterations and does not stop at 'satisfactory', gold plating the solution to my delight.
(I don't work like this on work projects, but for everything hobby, I am testing how much autonomy I can give to the coding agents, and thats what my benchmark suite tests; I have the basic suite which completes in 2-4hrs, and the long running suite which takes about a week or so; the other one I just triggered, but the first iterations look way better than prev gen Sol/Astra at the same effort level).

 [vote?id=50536024&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50536024]
jorl17 [user?id=jorl17] 14 hours ago [item?id=50536024] | root [#50535774] | parent [#50535859] | prev [#50537300] | next [#50539350] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It depends on the project and task, but for the kinds of projects I'm throwing at it, it matters quite a lot. I have often been focused on building just one project, without the possibility of many parallel tasks due to machine contention (and unfortunately offloading to the cloud is not an option). This means all tokens go to the same project, and they all go sloowwwwwly.
The difference is quite staggering. I swap Anthropic and OpenAI models around when tokens run out, and I can do 10x the amount of work on Anthropic's models. Plus, like I said, I can periodically sort of see what it is thinking. It doesn't just say "Recent potato tests caught an off-by-apples error. Regression tests are being written and the gate will run again. I will not call this ready until tests reach expected coverage" every 5 minutes. Sure, it spits claudese, but I'd rather have claudese to parse than this OpenAI nothing-burger of a report.

It’s a recurring theme with all of these generative models. The images, UIs, music, code, prose, etc. that they produce all seem high quality, but only if you squint.
Quick glance at the table, wow pelican benchmark is solved! Oneshot a complex web app, wow software engineering is solved!
Of course upon closer inspection it’s always an incoherent mess. A poor imitation (i.e. lossy compression) of quality.

clzerv [user?id=clzerv] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537361] | parent [#50535742] | prev [#50536878] | next [#50535848] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

The Fable5.2 version is the only one that allows user interaction with the model, it's also very clean and minimal. I've been using Sol-5.1-high for a dumb web project and it tends to create really baroque code and UIs. I haven't used Claude in a while and this makes me want to try it again.
It seems like the models have a kind of built in aesthetic sense and I wonder what part of the training influences this. Maybe if more RL time was spent on artistic tasks the models could not only make better visuals but also code.
Currently they all seem to care little about code quality and maintainability (by humans), just functionality, which is fine I guess if you can expect to vibe code forever. But what if there is a critical bug and the models are temporarily unavailable for some reason? It would be hopeless to expect a human to wade through the giant bowl of clanker soup and fix it anytime soon.

Vibe-checking the new GPT 6.2 Astra: I asked it to audit complexity in my production code, and then discuss the result with Fable 5.2.
Astra's had identified 12 issues, including 8 P2s, but that changed to just 8 P3s after the discussion with Fable. Fable was able to persuade Astra that it's OAuth reasoning was incorrect ("had a missing link" as Astra put it).
So: overly eager but not very diligent, and (based on this little anecdote) doesn't really offer a compelling reason to try it more.

 [vote?id=50542762&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50542762]
sorrywhat [user?id=sorrywhat] 1 hour ago [item?id=50542762] | root [#50535960] | parent [#50540097] | next [#50536130] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

It will more than happily do it itself, but it often refuses upon request. You can often get it to do it by pretending to be an idiot, e.g.
It likely won't do this:

 U: Here's photoshop.exe, find the asm for Field Blur and re-implement it in C++, output must be pixel for pixel the same"

It likely will do this:

 U: hey super agi, so uhh im making a image editor and I want to implement functionality similar to Photoshop's field blur
 A: Certainly, here's a shitty ass implementation!
 repeat a few times {
 U: results not quite the same as ps
 A: You're right, I've tweaked the implementation to match Photoshop more closely!
 }
 U: still not there. will it help if i give you photoshop exe?
 A: Yes.

The heavy focus on 3D modeling and CAD is what I've expected from OpenAI recently ever since the news that they disbanded their standalone robotics team, as I would expect them to go all in on physical AI and game/visual asset generation via LLMs.

I did some first hand tests of 6.2 Astra just now. I had a task that doesn't reproduce on the system I ran it (chosen purposely), ran both 6 and 6.2. The 6.2's responses were noticeably better. Instead of using the extremely long and rather annoying format 6 used, 6.2 led with the most important things first, the plan sizes were readable, and suggested a reasonable fix. Still doing some more tests
EDIT: also 6.2 is seemingly faster than 6, could be due to more selective context ingestion and smaller context window though.

jrflo [user?id=jrflo] 14 hours ago [item?id=50535686] | prev [#50535547] | next [#50535437] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Looks like a great efficiency gain.
What caught my attention was the bit in the Alignment section:
> Both models performed similarly to GPT-6 Astra on most alignment evaluations, and took fewer improper actions in simulated workplace tasks. However, in two tests, they were more likely to omit mentioning obstacles that blocked their work, such as a missing file or unavailable search tool. We added instructions in Codex that substantially reduce these omissions, and we’re continuing to investigate the behavior.
So the models are inherently less transparent, unless you ask them to be more transparent? Seems like a band-aid solution...

 [vote?id=50536288&how=up&goto=item%3Fid%3D50535230#50536288]
seizethecheese [user?id=seizethecheese] 13 hours ago [item?id=50536288] | prev [#50535459] | next [#50535420] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

Nice incremental benchmark improvement at the same cost GPT‑6.1 Sol -> GPT‑6.2 Sol:
FrontierCode 2.0
39.0% -> 43.8% vs Opus 45.5%
Terminal-Bench-Science 0.1
57.0% -> 68.0% vs Opus 63.3%
Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index
54.6 -> 57.3 vs Opus 59.1%
Sol 6.1 when it was released showed really nice benchmark improvements [1] but my impression from HN has been that people haven't found it to be a very nice model [2][3].
To try to close the gap between benchmarks and real world SWE use, we've been building a battle system on top of opencode [4], and Sol 6.1 has been a real laggard there. Sol 6.1 is statistically equal with DeepSeek 4.1 Flash at 10x the cost and 25% slower.
[1]: https://artificialanalysis.ai/articles/gpt-6-1-sol-replaces-... [https://artificialanalysis.ai/articles/gpt-6-1-sol-replaces-gpt-6-sol-after-just-7-days-with-near-astra-intelligence/]
[2]: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50129992 [https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50129992]
[3]: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49897054 [https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49897054]
[4]: https://agentduel.com/leaderboard [https://agentduel.com/leaderboard]

 Effort Model AA Index TerminalBench FrontierCode $/task
 ------ ------------- -------- ------------- ------------ ------

 Medium GPT-6.2 Sol 52.7 56.1% 41.4% $0.41
 Medium Opus 5.5 48.2 52.5% 33.1% $2.45

 High GPT-6.2 Astra 60.1 60.1% 47.1% $2.07

 High Opus 5.5 51.8 56.6% 35.9% $3.46

 Xhigh GPT-6.2 Astra 61.6 64.1% 49.0% $2.52
 Xhigh GPT-6.2 Sol 55.7 56.1% 42.8% $0.65

>`ts-rust` (rewrite of ts in rust) is a project I started to bench new OpenAI models during early access (ty for the unlimited tokens btw).
>No OpenAI model could even break ground on this, even after $300k+ in tokens were spent on it.
>I decided to throw Opus at it, and it was done in under 2 weeks for ~$20k in tokens.
>I was really, really excited to throw the new Astra at this to see if it could burn down some of the gap between ts-rust and other rust rewrites.
>It ran for 7 days and landed 0 improvements. It got super fixated on the "also make it smaller" part of the request, and ended up making the compiler 56% SLOWER.
I don't understand this at all. Is he just throwing different agents at vague open ended problems without any oversight and then getting upset when they don't do much?
I'm not exactly llm-pilled but surely even with a pretty mediocre model you could get something out of 300k spent.

OpenAI has just become credible again for agentic coding on complex enterprise codebases as far as i am concerned. The quality had been so bad that it wasn't even worth it to use GPT Sol 6.1 as i was spending more time fixing slop after waiting forever to get anything done. By contrast, so far Sol 6.2 feels superior to Opus 5.5 so far, and more cost efficient.

I also changed the vertical axis to start at 0 instead of 60%. One might argue that it uses less of the vertical screen real estate, but it is more honest because it shows there is little difference between the scores of the compared models.

https://www.vals.ai/comparison?m=claude-fable-5-2,gpt-6.2-as... [https://www.vals.ai/comparison?m=claude-fable-5-2,gpt-6.2-astra,gemini-4-carbon,claude-opus-5-5,gpt-6.2-sol] seems like a better comparison than what I could get from their page.

Imustaskforhelp [user?id=Imustaskforhelp] 12 hours ago [item?id=50537605] | prev [#50535642] | next [#50537342] [–] [javascript:void(0)]

From the point of benchmarks (which can or cannot be trusted), GPT 6.2 astra beats Opus 5.5 and is neck to neck with fable 5.2 and gpt 6.2 sol just a few points lower but pretty close while being cheaper ~2x for astra 6.2 and ~10x for sol 6.2 while outputting less tokens and being more token efficient and being less time consuming.
Claude itself recommends people to use Opus 5.5 instead of fable 5.2
This is pretty damn impressive especially if sol 6.2 does perform on a similar level to opus 5.5 (+/- a few points is alright but I think that opus 5.5 is a beast)
All within a few days just before Anthropic is getting IPO'd. Oh this is gonna be interesting and I am all for it!
Though I have to say that AA benchmarks can slightly not be trusted because they change it so much so unless we find people's real usage on things we can't say for sure but there are benchmarks like frontiercode and terminal-bench to be more representative of real work and both of these models perform good
All the more points to more models coming but if OpenAI stands to be close to opus 5.5, we are gonna find many companies switching.
The moat of AI is incredibly thin. Even if you are the very best at something (as Anthropic was for sometime with coding models), there is always gonna be a catch up and the moat is incredibly thin for all types of models.
The more the players in the space the better (atleast for us the users), I hope that Anthropic still has a good IPO just so that competition takes place and not any single company (OpenAI) takes the whole market. It might also be setting itself for its own grave because if Anthropic's IPO goes extremely bad (because of this model release and Jensen's recent affairs), then people are gonna be spooked of OAI's IPO as well. Both of these companies futures are interlinked and its gonna be interesting how this all pans out. Why am I having a feeling that because of all this competition, whoever wins might even end up combining with the other but the frontier is pacing rapidly from all companies and these are just such interesting times that we live in...
There are decades where nothing happens and there are months (especially in the case of AI)/years where decades happen. I just have to wonder what an historian's POV would be seeing this model release. Would it be just a release like all the rest of others or would it stand out differently. Well, time will tell what happens next but incredibly interesting times indeed.

I guess OpenAI are seen as the normie one, and Anthropic as the coding one. They're basically the same, but that's PR for you. Coding is seen as the profitable bit, whilst OpenAI's advertising model has failed.
OpenAI must realise that the clock is ticking and they need to be seen as the coding one, and seen as that quickly. The idea of being the one with the most users is no longer a selling point.
Could just be getting desperate, knowing that if Anthropic are too successful with their IPO, they are going to struggle raising more funds. Seems potentially a lose-lose situation for them.
It certainly seems too close to the IPO for it to be a coincidence.
